**Book One of the Blood Craft Series**

# **Chapter 1**

The moon followed us. I'd been watching it for the last twenty miles, that bloated October thing hanging too low in the sky, and I couldn't shake the feeling it watched back. Blood-red around the edges, like someone had dipped the lunar surface in crimson ink and let it bleed into the darkness. Mom said it was just atmospheric distortion, some trick of dust and light refracting through particulate matter in the upper atmosphere. She'd explained it three times now, each explanation more detailed and scientific than the last, as if she could logic away the unease crawling up my spine.

But my hands kept finding the armrest, fingers drumming a pattern I couldn't name. Three beats, pause, two beats, pause, repeat. An unconscious rhythm that felt older than me, like muscle memory from a life I'd never lived.

Twenty-three in four days. Halloween birthday—the joke had worn thin by the time I hit double digits. No, what bothered me wasn't the date. It was the number itself. Twenty-three. It sat in my gut like something I'd swallowed wrong, a weight that had been building for weeks now. Some primal instinct whispering that crossing that threshold would change everything.

I tried to shake it off. Told myself it was just quarter-life crisis bullshit, the anxiety of approaching real adulthood. But the feeling persisted, a low-frequency hum in my bones that intensified the closer we got to Broken Bow.

Dad's knuckles were white on the steering wheel—had been since we left West. The muscles in his forearms stood out like cables under skin, tendons flexing with each micro-adjustment. I'd never seen him this tense, not even during the tornado warning three years back when we'd huddled in the basement while the sky turned green overhead.

The two-lane blacktop unwound ahead of us, trees pressing close on both sides like spectators at a funeral procession. Pine and oak and something else I couldn't name, their branches forming a canopy that swallowed the starlight. The headlights carved a tunnel through the darkness, but it felt temporary, fragile. Like the night was just waiting for us to pass so it could close back in.

Somewhere in the back of my mind, a voice I didn't recognize whispered that we shouldn't have come.

I'd learned early to trust the dark more than the light. While other kids were waking up to cartoons and Frosted Flakes, I was finally crawling into bed, my internal clock wired backward since birth. The sun felt wrong to me—too bright, too exposing, like someone had turned on the house lights during a movie. But night? Night was home.

Dad called us creatures of the night. Always with that little pause, that fractional hesitation where his mouth would shape a word he didn't say. "It's in our blood," he'd add, and Mom would laugh about needing coffins instead of beds. Inside jokes that stopped being funny around the time I turned eighteen and started paying attention to the things they didn't say.

Like how they never invited people over after dark. How they always checked the locks three times before bed. How Mom's hand would drift to her collarbone when she was nervous, fingers tracing a pattern on skin that held no scars but seemed to remember some old wound.

I'd written it off as quirky parent shit. Everyone's family had their weird habits, right? But lately, I'd been noticing more. The way they'd go silent when I walked into a room mid-conversation. The locked drawer in Mom's study I'd tried to open as a kid and gotten grounded for a week. The fact that in twenty-three years, we'd never once had extended family visit. No grandparents, no aunts or uncles, no cousins at Thanksgiving.

When I'd asked about it once, Dad had said something about being estranged from his family, and Mom had changed the subject so fast I got whiplash. I'd let it drop. Figured maybe their families were assholes, or religious nutjobs who'd disowned them for some stupid reason.

Now, watching Dad's death grip on the wheel and Mom's hands twisting in her lap, I wondered if I should've pressed harder.

Funny thing about growing up nocturnal—you see things other people don't. The woods behind our house taught me that. I'd spent countless hours out there between the hours of midnight and dawn, not because I gave a damn about nature or had some deep spiritual connection to the earth. No, I went because 3 AM has a texture that daylight can't touch. 

The silence isn't really silent. It breathes. You can hear the world's heartbeat if you're patient enough—the rustle of a rabbit thirty yards away, the creak of branches settling, the almost subsonic hum of insects going about their tiny, vital business. And sometimes, when the wind died completely and the forest held its breath, I swear I could hear my own heartbeat echoing back from the trees.

More than once, I'd felt watched. Not the creepy stalker kind of watched—this was different. Ancient. Patient. Like the woods themselves had eyes and were taking my measure, deciding if I belonged. I'd mentioned it to Dad once, and he'd gotten this look on his face. Not dismissive, not mocking. Something else. Something that might've been recognition.

"Old places remember," he'd said, then changed the subject.

Mom never liked my nocturnal wanderings. "Night owl," she'd call me, same as Dad, but her voice always had an edge he didn't hear. Or pretended not to. Sharp with an anxiety she tried to hide behind smiles and subject changes. She'd asked me once, after I'd come back from a particularly long walk at 4 AM, if I'd ever seen anything unusual out there.

"Like what?" I'd asked.

"Anything that didn't belong. Anything that made you feel..." She'd trailed off, fingers doing that unconscious dance on her collarbone again. "Never mind. Just be careful, okay?"

I'd promised I would, not understanding what I was promising to be careful of. Now, in the backseat of the car with tension thick enough to choke on, that conversation felt like a warning I'd been too stupid to heed.

They'd joke about being vampires, about buying coffins—inside jokes that felt less funny the older I got. Dad would get this look when she said it, something flickering behind his eyes that was gone before I could name it. Guilt, maybe. Or fear. Or both twisted together into something I didn't have words for.

We were movie people—the kind who measured weeks in films rather than days. Three, sometimes six a week, the three of us crammed on the couch with popcorn going cold while we argued about whether the gore in *Hellraiser* was gratuitous or art. (Mom and I voted art; Dad remained unconvinced.) 

Mom and I had a thing for horror. The nastier the better. Blood, viscera, body horror that made normal people look away—that's where we leaned in. I'd caught her grinning during the hobbling scene in *Misery*, and we'd bonded over the practical effects in *The Thing*. Dad would groan and cover his eyes, but he never left the room.

"You two are sick," he'd say, but there was affection in it.

"We appreciate the craftsmanship," Mom would counter, and I'd nod sagely like we were discussing fine art instead of Rob Bottin's grotesque genius.

But I had a secret: I was a sucker for rom-coms. *The Proposal*, *The Ugly Truth*, *10 Things I Hate About You*—all that bullshit about love conquering impossible odds, about people finding each other despite everything working against them. I'd watch them alone, laptop screen tilted away from the door, volume low with headphones in, because Dad would've laughed himself sick if he'd caught me.

He was all about his sci-fi head trips—*Blade Runner*, *Arrival*, *Ex Machina*. Loved anything that made him think, that twisted reality into philosophical pretzels. And action blockbusters, the kind with no romance whatsoever. *Die Hard*, *Mad Max*, *John Wick*. Pure adrenaline and violence and men being stoic.

We met in the middle on the good stuff: *Fight Club*, *Queen of the Damned*, the extended editions of *Lord of the Rings*. Movies with teeth, with darkness that felt earned rather than gratuitous. Films that understood violence had consequences and love was complicated.

*Queen of the Damned* had been Mom's suggestion, back when I was sixteen and finally old enough to watch R-rated movies with them. I'd expected your standard vampire schlock—sparkles and angst and abstinence metaphors. What I got was Anne Rice's fever dream of blood and sensuality and the dark allure of immortality. Aaliyah's Akasha moving like liquid sin, Stuart Townsend's Lestat embodying everything dangerous and seductive about the vampire mythos.

I'd been obsessed with it for months. Read the books, consumed every vampire film I could find. *Let the Right One In*, *Interview with the Vampire*, even the terrible ones. There was something about the archetype that called to me—the immortality, sure, but more than that. The power. The freedom from human frailty. The idea that you could become something more than what you were born to be.

Mom had watched me go through that phase with an expression I couldn't decode. Not quite approval, not quite concern. Like she was waiting for me to reach some conclusion on my own.

"What is it about vampires that fascinates you?" she'd asked one night, after we'd finished rewatching *From Dusk Till Dawn*.

I'd thought about it, trying to articulate something that felt instinctive rather than intellectual. "I guess... they're free? Like, they exist outside all the normal rules. They're powerful, they live forever, they don't have to pretend to be something they're not."

"And the blood?" she'd pressed. "The need to take life to sustain their own?"

"It's just the price," I'd said. "Everything has a price, right? At least they're honest about what they are. They don't lie about their nature."

She'd gone quiet after that, and I'd felt like I'd said something significant without knowing what. Dad had cleared his throat and suggested we watch something lighter. We'd put on *The Princess Bride*, and the moment had passed.

What I didn't know then was that my taste in movies would end up being the least weird thing about me. That in a few hours, I'd be living in a world where vampires were real, where blood had power, and where the price for that power would be paid in ways I couldn't begin to imagine.


West, Texas. Population 2,807, according to the sign on Highway 77, though that number felt generous. If you counted the cemetery, maybe you'd break 3,000. Dad always said it felt like a town that time forgot, which is exactly why he loved it. A place where you could disappear into normal, into quiet, into the kind of unremarkable life where nobody asked questions because nobody cared enough to ask.

Mom and Dad moved there in the '90s, before I was born, during that weird post-grunge era when everyone was flannel and angst. They bought fifteen acres of land cheap from a Czech farmer's widow, built a house at the end of a dirt road where the nearest neighbor was three miles away through pine forest. Close enough to town to not be total hermits, far enough out to have real privacy.

Czech heritage, Westfest every Labor Day, kolaches so good they'd make you weep—that was West. The kind of town where everyone knew everyone, where your third-grade teacher remembered your grandmother, where newcomers were tolerated as long as they kept their heads down and didn't make waves.

My parents had been keeping their heads down for twenty-five years when we took that drive. Twenty-five years of quiet. Of normal. Of carefully constructed ordinariness that I'd never thought to question.

But now, pieces were clicking together in ways I didn't like. The isolation. The homeschooling. The nocturnal lifestyle that Dad had normalized for me but that I'd realized in college was deeply weird. Most people weren't wired backward. Most people didn't feel more alive at 3 AM than noon.

Most people's parents didn't flinch at unknown phone numbers like they'd heard a gunshot.

Dad was a programmer—freelance, worked from home, which meant the house always smelled like coffee and coconut LaCroix and sounded like keyboard clicks at 2 AM. He'd built his career in the early days of the internet, during the dot-com boom, and had been smart enough to go independent before the bubble burst. Coded for companies that needed someone good enough to work remotely, back when that was still unusual.

He made enough to keep us comfortable without being flashy. We weren't rich, but we weren't hurting. And more importantly, he never had to leave the property for work. Never had to show his face in an office, never had to play corporate politics or attend conferences or do any of the normal networking shit that career advancement usually required.

I'd thought he was just introverted. An antisocial nerd who'd found the perfect career for his personality. Now I wondered if there were other reasons he'd structured his life to minimize exposure to the outside world.

Mom homeschooled me. Said the public system would crush my "creative spirit," though I suspected she just didn't like the idea of strangers raising me, shaping me, teaching me things she hadn't vetted first. I didn't mind. The isolation that should've damaged me somehow didn't. Maybe because I had the internet, had online friends and communities that felt more real than any physical classroom could've been.

She'd taught me well—better than well. By the time I hit college age, I'd tested out of most of my gen-eds and gone straight into upper-division computer science courses. Math came easy to me. Logic, patterns, systems. The clean binary certainty of code where things either worked or they didn't.

If only life were that simple.

I'd taken a gap year before starting college, ostensibly to help around the property and "find myself" in that vague way gap year kids always claimed to. Really, I just wasn't ready to leave yet. Wasn't ready to separate from them, to step into the wider world and all its demands. We'd been a unit my whole life—the three of us against the world—and breaking that felt wrong.

So I stayed. Helped Dad build a new deck, learned to properly maintain the property, spent long nights talking with Mom about everything and nothing. She'd taught me to cook that year. Real cooking, not just bachelor pad survival meals, but the kind of food that takes time and attention and love.

"You need to know how to take care of yourself," she'd said, guiding my hands through the motion of kneading bread dough. "How to nourish yourself properly. It's important."

At the time, I'd thought she meant I needed to not live off ramen in college. Now I wondered if she'd known she wouldn't always be there to take care of me. If she'd been preparing me for something neither of us wanted to name.

College came eventually—community college first, then transfer to a state school. Computer Science degree, almost done. One semester left. I'd stretched it out longer than most, taking minimum courseloads and online classes when I could, never quite ready to fully commit to being away from home.

I'd commute when I had to, crash at friends' places when the drive got too tiresome, but I always came back. Always pulled back into the gravity well of home, of family, of the only life I'd ever known.

Dad never pushed me to hurry up and graduate. Never gave me shit about taking so long or suggested I needed to get on with my life. If anything, he seemed relieved every time I came back. Like he needed me there as much as I needed to be there.

We had time, I'd thought. No rush. They were only in their late forties—young enough, healthy enough. We'd have years to figure out what came next, decades to slowly separate as I built my own life.

Or so I'd thought.

What college did give me was women. And with women came the discovery of who I actually was underneath all the isolation and awkwardness.

Before college, my romantic life was a desert—dry, empty, embarrassing in its complete lack of anything interesting to report. I'd had exactly one girlfriend in high school, a girl I'd met through an online gaming clan who lived two towns over. We'd dated for four months, fumbled through some truly awkward making out, and mutually agreed we were better as friends.

That was it. The sum total of my romantic experience before freshman year.

But college opened doors I didn't know existed. Turned out when you weren't the weird homeschooled kid with no social skills, when you were just another Computer Science major in a sea of nerds, people paid attention. And when those people discovered I could actually hold a conversation, had a sense of humor that wasn't just video game references, and knew how to dress like I gave a shit—well.

Turns out I had a taste for things that didn't fit in the vanilla box most people lived in.

It started innocently enough. A girl in my Data Structures class, Sarah, mentioned she was into BDSM during a casual conversation about dating horror stories. She'd said it so casually, like it was just another preference like liking tall guys or having a thing for musicians. I'd been curious enough to ask questions, mature enough to actually listen to the answers.

She'd invited me to a munch—a casual meetup at a coffee shop for people in the local kink community. No play, no scenes, just conversation with people who happened to share certain interests. I'd gone expecting to feel like a tourist, an outsider looking in on something alien.

Instead, I'd felt like I'd come home.

Dominance came naturally to me in ways I hadn't expected. Not the toxic masculine bullshit version, not the insecure asshole who needed to control everything because he was afraid. The real thing. The kind that came from confidence, from genuinely wanting to take care of someone, from finding satisfaction in another person's pleasure and submission.

I read everything I could find. First, the basics—consent, negotiation, safewords, the difference between fantasy and reality. Then deeper—the psychology of power exchange, the trust required for real D/s relationships, the care and responsibility that came with dominance. I attended workshops, listened to people who'd been in the lifestyle for decades, learned from their experience.

The BDSM community in Austin welcomed me like I'd been missing my whole life. For the first time, I was around people who understood that you could want things that seemed contradictory to outsiders. That you could be both gentle and fierce, nurturing and demanding, that power could be a gift given rather than something taken.

And then there were the cat girls.

It started as a joke—someone had posted a meme in one of the Discord servers I frequented, something about "goth gf vs cat girl gf," and I'd commented that cat girls were underrated. What followed was a half-serious, half-joking conversation about why cat ears and collars were hot, about the appeal of that specific kind of playful submission mixed with feline independence.

I'd laughed it off at the time. Figured it was just funny internet nonsense, the kind of thing you didn't take seriously.

Then I met Jenna.

She'd been a friend of Sarah's, showed up at a private play party wearing black cat ears and a collar with a little bell. We'd talked for hours—about anime and video games and horror movies, about philosophy and what we wanted from life. And when she'd asked if I wanted to scene with her, when she'd dropped into that playful, teasing headspace that was part kitten and part brat, something had clicked.

It wasn't just the aesthetic, though I'd be lying if I said that wasn't part of it. It was the energy—playful but submissive, independent but needy, fierce but soft. The way she'd nuzzle into my hand when I'd done something she liked, the way she'd arch and hiss when she was being playful-defiant. The way she'd purr—actually purr—when I'd petted her hair just right.

We'd dated for eight months, and it had been good. Really good. But we'd wanted different things long-term. She'd been graduating and moving to California for a tech job, and neither of us wanted to do long distance. We'd ended things amicably, stayed friends, and I'd been left with the certainty that I had a very specific type.

Sitting in the backseat with my parents three feet away, fantasies about collars and cat ears and girls who purred when you scratched behind their ears weren't exactly appropriate. I forced my attention to the window, to the darkness rolling past. Trees like prison bars. Stars that looked too bright, too close. That red moon still following us, patient as death.

I tried to calculate how many hours until I could get some privacy in the cabin. Maybe take a walk alone, clear my head, maybe handle the semi that was threatening to become embarrassing. But the tension in the car kept pulling me back to reality, kept reminding me that something was wrong.

My parents weren't acting normal. And whatever had them this spooked, it was getting worse the closer we got to our destination.

This trip was my idea. My birthday present to myself—four days until I turned twenty-three on Halloween, and instead of parties or bar crawls or the kind of celebration most people my age craved, I wanted this: a cabin in Broken Bow, Oklahoma. Just us. Woods, water, silence. A return to simplicity.

Mom had loved the idea immediately, her face lighting up in that way it did when something genuinely pleased her. "Perfect," she'd said. "Just the three of us, like old times."

Dad had hesitated—he always did when it came to leaving the house, leaving our carefully controlled little world. But Mom could talk him into anything, and I'd wanted it badly enough to push. To insist. To use my birthday as leverage in ways I now regretted.

"It'll be good for us," I'd argued. "When's the last time we actually went somewhere together?"

He couldn't argue with that. Couldn't come up with a reason to say no that wouldn't sound paranoid or controlling. So he'd agreed, and Mom had booked the cabin, and I'd spent two weeks being excited about something that now felt like the worst idea I'd ever had.

Broken Bow Lake had looked perfect in the photos. Deep water, old trees, mountains that had seen things. The kind of place where you could disappear for a weekend and come back different. 

I'd been counting on it. Counting on the change, on the space from normal life, on getting to spend time with my parents before I finally graduated and moved on to whatever came next. Quality time. Making memories.

The kind of memories I'd want to keep.

Now, watching the trees close in and the moon turn redder and my parents' fear become palpable, I wondered what the fuck I'd been thinking. You don't tempt fate by leaving safety. You don't invite change when change might be the last thing you survive.

My phone buzzed. The sound was too loud in the silence, too sharp. 254 area code—local to West. Unknown number.

In the front seat, Mom and Dad both went rigid. Not the gradual tension of worry building—this was instantaneous. Muscles locking, breath catching. The way prey animals freeze when they catch a predator's scent.

Just for a second, but I caught it. The way Dad's shoulders tensed, drawing up toward his ears. The way Mom's hand found the door handle like she was bracing for impact, knuckles gone bloodless white. The way they looked at each other in the mirror, some wordless communication passing between them that I couldn't parse.

I should've let it go to voicemail. Should've listened to that instinct that was screaming danger. Should've noticed that unknown numbers didn't usually call at 9 PM on a Friday night unless something was wrong.

But I was twenty-two, not quite twenty-three, and I hadn't learned yet that some calls change everything.

"Good evening, Riven Sixxx speaking," I said in my best British accent—a stupid habit I'd picked up for spam calls, a bit of theater to make the mundane interesting. "How may I help you, mate?"

Silence. Not the empty kind where the line's dead. Not the robocall pause before the recorded message starts. This was different.

This was the listening kind.

Someone was on the other end. I could hear it in the quality of the quiet, in the faint sound of breath being carefully controlled. Someone was holding the phone, waiting, measuring. Hunting.

The hair on my arms stood up. Every instinct I had screamed wrong, wrong, wrong. The silence stretched, became heavy with intention. With threat.

Then the line went dead.

Not the normal click of someone hanging up. This was abrupt, violent. Like the connection had been severed rather than ended.

"Spam call," Mom said too quickly, forcing a smile that didn't reach her eyes. Her voice was an octave too high, words coming too fast. The kind of lie that screamed I'm lying but pretending I'm not.

I looked at Dad in the rearview mirror. His knuckles had gone white again, bloodless with pressure. His jaw was clenched so tight I could see the muscle jumping. "Another one, yeah," he said, and laughed, but it sounded wrong. Hollow. Like laughter shaped by muscle memory but stripped of all actual humor.

"Tech-free weekend, remember?" His eyes found mine in the mirror, and there was pleading there. Desperate. "Should probably turn that thing off."

He winked at me, trying to sell it, trying to act like everything was normal and fine and there was nothing to worry about. But I saw what was behind his eyes now. Saw past the mask he'd been wearing my entire life.

Fear. 

Not anxiety. Not worry. Real fear. The primal kind that came from knowing something was hunting you and getting closer.

And in that moment, I knew. Knew that whatever they'd been hiding from me, whatever they'd been protecting me from, it had found us. The phone call wasn't random. It was a message. A declaration.

We see you. We know where you are. We're coming.

"Where are we now?" I asked, trying to inject some normalcy back into the conversation, trying to pretend I hadn't just watched my parents' carefully constructed masks crack. I peered out the window as the trees closed in around us, shrouded in shadows. The night sky blossomed above us with stars—a dazzling tapestry of light that seemed infinitely deeper than I was used to, unpolluted by city lights or suburban glow.

Out here, in the middle of nowhere Oklahoma, you could see the Milky Way as a river of light across the darkness. Could see planets with the naked eye, satellites drifting in their slow orbits, the occasional meteor scratching a brief line across infinity. It should've been beautiful. Peaceful.

Instead, it felt like being watched by a thousand eyes.

The moon cast its soft glow across the landscape, illuminating our path in shades of silver and blood-red. Trees threw long shadows across the road, dark shapes that seemed to reach for the car as we passed.

"We're about an hour from the cabin," Dad replied, his voice filled with forced excitement as he turned down the radio, cutting off Frank Sinatra mid-croon. The sudden silence felt ominous, like we'd just turned off the last thing keeping the dark at bay.

After a brief moment where the only sound was tires on asphalt and my own pulse in my ears, Dad turned back to me, attempting casual interest. "Once we get there and unpack, are you planning to go to sleep right away, or are you going to stay up for a bit?"

It was such a normal question. Such a parent thing to ask. Like we weren't all feeling the weight of whatever was coming, like that phone call hadn't just changed everything.

I played along, tapping my finger against my lips, pretending to contemplate the question seriously. "I think I want to explore the area," I said after a pause that felt too long. "The night sky will be spectacular out here, free from city lights. You can really see the stars shining clearer than ever."

It was true—my heart raced at the thought of stargazing in the great outdoors, of having that direct connection to the infinite that city living never quite allowed. A fitting way to celebrate my upcoming birthday, away from the usual noise and bustle of life. Away from everything familiar and safe.

With a glimmer of hope in her eyes that looked too desperate to be real, my mother turned to me and softly pleaded, "Could I join you on this walk? It sounds absolutely delightful, especially after enduring three long hours of Sinatra's crooning during the drive."

She gestured with her hands, sweeping them wide from her head as if to express just how overwhelmed she felt, her arms rising like they were trying to embrace the very joy she sought. But I saw her fingers trembling. Saw the way her smile didn't quite work right, like she'd forgotten how to make her face do what she wanted.

I forced a laugh, nearly snorting as I playfully replied, "I wouldn't mind that at all, Mom. Dad can start cooking on the grill as a fitting punishment for his stubborn choice of music on the way here."

The joke felt hollow in my mouth, but I needed it. Needed to pretend everything was fine, that we were just a normal family on a normal trip, that nothing had changed with one silent phone call.

With a mischievous grin that looked more like a grimace, Dad leaned back in his seat, launching into his best—and absolutely terrible—imitation of Dean Winchester from *Supernatural*. "Driver picks the music, and shotgun shuts their cake hole," he declared in a deep, dramatic voice that would've made Jensen Ackles weep with embarrassment.

We all laughed. Had to laugh. The sound rang false in the enclosed space, desperate and brittle, but we maintained it. Held onto it like a lifeline. Proof that we were still us, still a family, still capable of joy even as something dark and terrible closed in from all sides.

The warmth of the moment wrapped around us like a blanket with holes in it—there but insufficient, comforting but failing to actually provide comfort. The shared humor created a bond that felt more like chains now. We chuckled together for what felt like a solid minute, the sound of our forced laughter mingling with the rustle of the trees outside the car windows.

Eventually, the music faded back into the background, Sinatra returning with "The Way You Look Tonight," and a loaded silence enveloped us. Not peaceful. Not happy. Just… waiting. Filled with the subtle hum of the road and the promise of—what? A relaxing weekend? Or something else entirely?

None of us noticed the darkness that seemed to gather at the edge of the headlights, thickening into something almost tangible. None of us saw the way the trees had stopped moving, how the wind had died completely. None of us paid attention to how the moon above us had shifted from silver to crimson, like blood spreading through water.

And none of us realized that the cabin we were heading toward—the isolated, perfect, beautiful cabin I'd chosen for my birthday—was exactly the kind of place where nobody would hear you scream.

# **Chapter 2**

Fifteen minutes of silence.

That's how long it had been since the music died. Not faded out—died. One second Sinatra was crooning, the next there was nothing but the sound of tires on asphalt and my own breathing, too loud in my ears.

Dad and Mom were whispering. Urgent, clipped words I couldn't quite catch. I saw "they found us" shape itself on Dad's lips, though that made no fucking sense. Who? Found us where?

"How could they even know where we are?" Mom's voice cracked on the last word.

"The phone call." Dad's hands were strangling the steering wheel. "Had to be."

Then the world went dark.

Not night-dark. This was absolute—like someone had thrown a tarp over reality. The temperature dropped twenty degrees in a heartbeat. I could see my breath. Smell ozone and wet earth and something else, something that made my hindbrain scream run.

The impact came from above.

Something massive slammed into the roof of the car, and we were weightless, airborne, spinning. Metal screamed. Glass exploded. I hit the ceiling—no, the floor—no, the window—my body a pinball bouncing in a machine made of steel and my parents' screaming.

My watch was beeping. Heart rate 165. 170. 180.

Then black.

Without warning, we were plunged into an impenetrable darkness as though a vast, heavy curtain had been drawn across the
sky, swallowing the night whole. The temperature in the car plummeted, and I could see my breath misting in the sudden
cold. Then came a sound—a deep, grinding rumble that seemed to come from the earth itself. Suddenly, a
deafening thud as something collided violently with the car, jerking us from our path, the tires momentarily losing
their grip on the asphalt. A frisson of fear raced down my spine, and the hair on the back of my neck and arms stood on
end, my senses heightening in response to the jolt that surged through the vehicle and into my very core. The smell of
ozone filled the air, sharp and electric, mixing with something else—something ancient and earthen.

My parents' faces contorted in a mosaic of emotions—anxious glances, furrowed brows, and shadows of guilt intertwined
with palpable fear. In that moment, I saw them differently than I ever had before: not as the protective parents who had
sheltered me all my life, but as people carrying secrets too heavy to bear. The realization hit me like a physical blow,
stealing the breath from my lungs. Every childhood memory suddenly shifted, took on new meaning—all those times Dad called
us "creatures of the night," Mom's jokes about vampires, their carefully controlled lives in isolation. Had I ever truly
known them at all?

Then, my mother turned to me with an intensity so profound it sent chills coursing through me. Her eyes—had they always
been that shade of amber, liquid gold catching the strange light, or was this the first time I was truly seeing her?—
glistened with unshed tears that tracked silver down her cheeks. She reached back, her hand finding mine, squeezing with a
desperate strength that bordered on pain. "No matter what happens, know we love you and did our best to protect you from
what is coming," she declared, her voice steady yet laced with urgency, with finality. The words settled over me like a
shroud, heavy with goodbye.

"Mom, what are you talking about?" The words came out strangled, my throat constricting around them. A whirlwind of panic
spiraled within me, drowning rational thought. "You're scaring me! What the fuck is going on? What is coming and why? How
does a random spam call connect to any of this?" I found myself shouting, desperation rising in my tone, overwhelmed by
confusion. My hands shook, fingers digging into the leather seat. The normal rules of my world felt shattered, especially
when my parents didn't even flinch at my frantic exclamations—as if they'd been expecting my terror, preparing for this
moment my entire life.

My mind raced, a cacophony of questions spiraling like a tornado, each one fragmenting into more questions, more doubts,
more fear. The familiar interior of our car suddenly felt alien, as if I'd woken into a stranger's nightmare. My
heartbeat pounded in my ears, a frantic drum against my ribs. Just then, my watch emitted a series of sharp beeps,
pulling my attention to its glowing face. Alarmingly, the heartbeat sensor indicated my pulse was soaring beyond its
normal range—165, 170, climbing.  Before I could fully
grasp what was happening, I was shaken by a roaring sound that mixed with an unsettling rumble as the car lurched
skyward, airborne as if caught in a cyclone. In an instant, my body was thrust against the roof, and everything faded
into an abyss of darkness.

When I woke up, I was drowning in red.

Not metaphor. Actual red. Blood everywhere—the seats, the ceiling, the windows. It was in my mouth. Copper and salt. I gagged, spat, tried to breathe through the metallic stench and failed.

The car was on its side. I was still in the back seat, somehow. Everything was wrong-way-up. The ringing in my ears was so loud I wanted to claw it out of my skull. Gasoline. I smelled gasoline. And under that, burned meat.

The moon stared down through the shattered windshield. Blood-red. Of course it was.

"Riven." Mom's voice cut through the ringing. Weak but urgent. "Are you okay?"

I was okay. I was not okay. I didn't know. "Where's Dad?"

"The glovebox," she said, ignoring my question. "Get what's inside. Now."

"Where is—"

"NOW!"

The command hit me like a slap. Mom never yelled. Ever. I blinked blood out of my eyes and looked at the driver's seat.

Empty.

The door was gone. Not hanging open—gone. Torn off the hinges. The windshield looked like something had punched through it from the inside. Diamonds of safety glass glittered in the moonlight like scales.

"Mom, where—"

"The glovebox, Riven. We don't have time."

I started climbing over the seat. Every movement sent pain lancing through my ribs, my shoulder, my—everything. Then I got a good look at her in the moonlight, and my world narrowed to a single point of horror.

She wasn't pinned.

She was impaled.

Stone spikes. Three of them. Each one thick as my forearm, driven through the dashboard, through the seat, through her. One through her chest. One through her abdomen. One through her right thigh.

They weren't random. They were placed. Surgical. Deliberate.

"Mom—"

"The glovebox." Blood bubbled at the corner of her mouth when she spoke. "There's a box. Red velvet. Bring it to me."

My hands wouldn't stop shaking. I couldn't look away from the spike in her chest, from the way it had punched through her sternum and out her back, pinning her to the seat like a butterfly in a collector's case.

This wasn't an accident. This was murder.

"Mom, we need to—I'll call 911, I'll—"

"No time." Her voice was weakening. I could hear the liquid in her lungs, the way her breath rattled. "Please, baby. The box. Before he comes back."

He. Someone had done this. Was still here.

My heart raced as I reached for the glove box, my fingers trembling violently. The mystery of what lay inside beckoned to
me through the fog of denial still clinging to my thoughts.

"In the back, a small metal case, inside there's a vial; please, grab it." Her voice was a faint whisper, laced
with pain and urgency—but also love, so much love it hurt worse than any physical wound. How was she still speaking? How
was she still here with me? I fumbled in the dark confines of the cramped vehicle, my heart racing as I sifted through
the chaos, desperate to find the vial. Each labored breath she took seemed to pull the air from my own lungs.

"Take the vial… and get out of the car. Find a woman named Raechelle; she will help you. Get away from here, go home,
and look for my journal. It will explain everything." Her voice faltered, growing weaker with each word, yet she
summoned the strength to continue. Every instruction felt like goodbye. "Remember to always trust the signs and listen to
your heart. The magic in your blood will guide you when nothing else can." 

Magic? Blood? None of this made sense, but I couldn't ask, couldn't interrupt, because somewhere deep inside I knew—this
was the last time I'd hear her voice.

A single tear traced down her cheek, leaving a glistening trail through the dust and blood on her face. "I love you,
Riven. More than you'll ever know. Make us proud." Her gaze bore into mine, a mixture of desperation and resignation, as
if she was trying to memorize my face one final time, to carry it with her wherever she was going. The intensity of that
look felt like a brand on my soul.

I could feel tears stinging my eyes, hot and immediate, as I finally wrapped my trembling fingers around the cool metal
of the case. "Mom, I can't—please don't—" I opened my mouth to beg, to bargain, to promise anything if she'd just stay,
but the moment my eyes locked onto hers, I was met with something that shattered me completely.

Her eyes were distant now, looking through me rather than at me. The light that had always been there—the warmth, the
recognition, the unconditional love—was fading like stars at dawn. I watched it slip away, second by agonizing second,
and there was nothing I could do to stop it. The chilling emptiness that crept into her gaze signaled she was slipping
away from me, and the world tilted on its axis.

"No! Mom! MOM!" The scream tore from my throat, raw and primal. This wasn't happening. This couldn't be happening.

I found it. The metal case. Red velvet box inside. A vial of something dark that caught the moonlight wrong.

Mom's eyes were empty. Not dead—I'd know if she was dead, right? I'd feel it—but going. Leaving. The light draining out like water through a sieve.

"Mom—MOM!"

Gasoline. The smell hit me like a fist. The car was leaking, had been leaking, the whole time we'd been—

Hands grabbed me from behind. Yanked me out through the broken window. I hit the ground hard enough to see stars, and then the world went white-hot and LOUD as the car exploded. Heat washed over me in a wave. The smell of burning upholstery, melting plastic, cooking meat.

Mom.

I tried to crawl back toward the flames. Had to get her. Had to—

Someone kicked me flat. Not hard. Just enough. When I looked up, gasping, choking on smoke and grief, I saw her.

Hoodie. Dark jeans. Face in shadow. But her eyes—Jesus Christ, her eyes—glowed red like embers. Like the moon. Like the blood on my hands.

"You fucking asshole!" The scream tore out of me. "My mom was still in there!"

"She was already dead." The woman's voice was flat. Matter-of-fact. Like she was commenting on the weather. "I couldn't hear her heartbeat anymore."


"Your father fought," she said. Flat. Clinical. "He lost. And if I hadn't pulled you from that car, you'd be ash."

The words should have hurt. They did hurt. But beneath the pain, something else—truth I couldn't argue with.

"Who the fuck are you?" The vial was still in my hand. Metal case digging into my palm. "You know my name. You know about this—" I held up the vial. "You knew my mom."

"What did she tell you?" The woman's voice changed. Sharper. Interested.

My throat was raw from screaming. "Said to drink it. Find someone named Raechelle. Go home, read her journal." I was babbling, shock making me stupid. "What is it?"

She tilted her head. Red eyes caught the moonlight. "It'll change everything. Your mother's dying wish—drink it, embrace what you are, or throw it away and pretend tonight never happened."

"That's not an answer."

"It's the only one you're getting." She stepped closer. "Choose, Riven. Revenge, or oblivion?"

I looked at the vial. Dark liquid, almost black. Silver filigree on the case, pentagram seal on the cap. Mom's symbol. She'd prepared this. Waited for this. Died for this.

I opened the case.

The vial was simple. Glass, dark liquid, pentagram seal. Mom's work. I unscrewed the cap and the edge bit into my thumb. Blood welled up, dripped into the vial.

For a second—just a second—the liquid glowed.

The woman watched. Said nothing.

Fuck it.

I drank.

It tasted like copper. Like licking a battery. Like blood and metal and something older than both. I swallowed, gagged, forced it down.

Nothing happened.

I looked at her. "What was supposed to—"

Then it hit.

Not gradual. Not gentle. Like a freight train to the chest. Every nerve in my body lit up at once—pain, pleasure, I couldn't tell the difference anymore. My vision went white, then red, then colors I didn't have names for.

I was on the ground. When did I fall?

The world was too loud. I could hear the woman's heartbeat, steady and slow. Could hear insects in the trees, their legs scraping bark. Could hear my own blood rushing through veins that suddenly felt too small, too fragile.

Too human.

My hands. Something was wrong with my hands. The nails—longer, darker, sharper. I watched them change, watched my skin pale, watched veins darken beneath the surface like a road map drawn in ink.

"What—" My voice sounded different. Deeper. Wrong. "What the fuck did you give me?"

The power surge came from nowhere. One second I was on my hands and knees, trying not to vomit. The next, energy exploded out of me like a grenade. The ground shook. Trees bent. The car's wreckage rattled.

The woman stumbled back. First time she'd moved like that—surprised, off-balance.

"What are you?" Her voice had lost its flat certainty.

"You tell me." My voice came out wrong. Deeper. Like someone else was using my throat. "You're the one with all the answers."

She stared. "You're not human. Not anymore. But what you are..." She trailed off. "I don't know."

That should have terrified me. It didn't. What terrified me was how good I felt.

The pain from the crash—gone. The cuts, the bruises, the broken ribs I'd felt crack when I hit the car ceiling—gone. My vision was sharp enough to count leaves on trees fifty yards away. I could hear her breathing, smell her sweat, sense the pulse of her blood beneath her skin.

And I was starving.

Not hungry. Starving. A need so deep it made my jaw ache. My gums hurt. Something was wrong with my teeth.

"Who are you?" The question came out as a growl.

The woman dropped to one knee. Head bowed. "My name is Raechelle."

Raechelle. The name Mom had said. Find Raechelle.

"Stand up." The words came out like a command. I didn't mean them that way—didn't mean to sound like I was ordering her—but she shot to her feet like I'd yanked a chain.

She gasped. A small sound, caught between surprise and something else. Something that made my newly-sharp senses prickle with awareness.

"What the hell was that?" I backed up a step. "What did I do?"

"You commanded me." Her voice was steadier now. Almost impressed. "The bond between a familiar and her master allows for certain... compulsions."

"I'm not your fucking master."

"You are, actually." She pulled back her hood.

I'd seen her eyes already—red, glowing. But now I saw the rest. Black hair, pale skin, sharp features that should've been cold but weren't. She was younger than I'd thought. Or looked younger. With supernatural shit, who knew?

"I'm bound to the Sixxx bloodline," she said. "Have been for centuries. The vial you drank—that was your inheritance. Blood elixir. It activates the magic in your DNA."

"Magic." The word tasted like ash. "You're telling me magic is real."

"You just grew claws and can hear my heartbeat from ten feet away. Yes, magic is real."

Fair point.

"Most awakenings show one color," she continued. "One discipline of magic based on affinity. Yours showed all of them. Every single discipline. I've never seen that before."

The hunger gnawed at me. "What am I?"

"Blood Archon. That's your title, your legacy. You control blood—your own, others', the life force itself. But what you displayed..." She trailed off. "I don't know. Something more."

I was still reeling. "This is insane. You're telling me magic exists, I'm some kind of blood wizard—"

"Blood Archon."

"—whatever, and you've been tied to my family for centuries?" My voice was rising again. The air around me felt charged. "This can't be real. What was in that vial? LSD? Some kind of designer drug?"

"You weren't drugged." She was trying to stay calm, but I could hear her pulse quicken. "Your mother's journal will explain everything. You need to read it in her words."

The mention of Mom hit like a gut punch. The journal. She'd told me to find it. Before the spikes, before the fire, before everything turned to ash.

"We need to leave," Raechelle said. "Someone will have seen the explosion. Felt your awakening. We can't be here when they arrive."

"Who's 'they'?"

"Later. We need to move. Now."

"Why do you keep calling me 'Sir'?" The formality grated on me. Wrong, in all this wrongness.

"Because that's what you are. My master, by blood and bond."

"Don't." The word came out sharper than I meant. "My name is Riven. Use it."

She hesitated. "As you wish... Riven."

Better. Barely.

"What are you?" I asked. "You said centuries. You're not human."

She met my eyes. "No. I'm not."

She closed her eyes briefly, and I saw something flicker across her features—not the predatory hunger I might have feared,
but something more complex. Vulnerability, maybe. Longing. As if my very presence affected her as much as hers affected
me. When she inhaled deeply, it seemed less like a threat and more like someone seeking comfort in a familiar, long-lost
scent.

"I know you're overwhelmed," she continued, her voice gentler now, less formal. "I know you're scared and grieving and
nothing makes sense. But you're not alone in this, Riven. You were never meant to be alone." There was something in the
way she said it—a weight of years, perhaps centuries, of her own solitude—that made me believe her.

She pulled her hood back.

Red eyes—I'd seen those already. But now I saw the rest. Black hair, pale skin, freckles across her cheeks and nose like scattered paint. She was beautiful in a way that didn't need cataloging. The kind of face that would've stopped me cold on a normal night, in a normal world.

This was neither.

"We should go," she said again.

The exhaustion hit me like a truck. One second I was coherent, the next I could barely keep my eyes open.

"You need to rest," Raechelle said. "Your body's been through hell."

"Can't." My words were slurring. "Every time I close my eyes, I see—"

"I know." She hesitated. "I can help. Make it easier to sleep. Nothing permanent, just... a push."

I should've been alarmed. Wasn't. "Okay."

Her fingers touched my temple. Warmth spread through me, honey-slow. The tension I'd been carrying since the phone call melted away.

"Sleep, Riven. You're safe."

Darkness took me.


## *Raechelle's POV*

Three hundred years.

That's how long I'd been waiting for this night. Three centuries of watching, protecting, preparing for the moment when the blood would call and everything would change. Three hundred years of knowing exactly how this would end, and being powerless to stop it.

I felt it the moment Terravos crossed into their territory—a wrongness in the air, like reality itself recoiling from his presence. The Earth Archon didn't bother with subtlety. Why would he? He was ancient, powerful, and utterly convinced of his righteousness. In his mind, he was delivering justice. Ending a threat that should have died three centuries ago.

He had no idea that the real threat was just beginning to wake up.

I'd been shadowing them for the last hour, keeping to the treeline, moving through the darkness with the ease of someone who'd spent lifetimes hunting and being hunted. The Sixxx family car was easy to track—Marcus drove like someone who knew he was being pursued but was too human to do anything about it. The terror rolling off him was thick enough to taste, and Elara's desperation was a tangible thing, sharp and bitter.

But it was the boy—Riven—who drew my attention. His scent hit me from a hundred yards away, and I'd nearly stumbled. Not possible. After all these years, after all the careful planning and preparation, I wasn't prepared for the reality of him.

Blood Archon heritage, yes. I'd expected that. But underneath it, threaded through his very essence like gold through quartz, was something else. Something older. Something that made my instincts scream both predator and prey, danger and destiny, run and submit.

The scent of the original Blood Archon himself.

Not reincarnation—that would've been obvious, immediate. This was subtler. Like an echo that had been growing stronger with each generation, a genetic memory slowly surfacing. The mad Archon's essence, dormant for three hundred years, finally finding purchase in a descendant strong enough to bear it.

And in four days, on his twenty-third birthday, it would fully awaken.

Unless Terravos killed him first.

The phone call had been the Earth Archon's way of announcing himself—a courtesy he didn't owe but felt compelled to give. In the old days, before the treaties and councils and careful political dances, Archons had rules. You didn't ambush another Archon's bloodline without warning. You gave them a chance to surrender, to submit, to accept judgment.

Terravos was old-fashioned that way. It would be his undoing, but he didn't know that yet.

I watched from the shadows as the family's fear intensified, as Marcus's knuckles went white on the steering wheel and Elara's hand crept to her throat in that unconscious gesture I'd seen a thousand times. She knew what was coming. Had known for twenty-three years. Every moment of peace had been borrowed time, and now the debt was due.

The boy—I couldn't think of him as Riven yet, not when he was so painfully unaware of what he was—tried to lighten the mood with jokes and laughter. The sound of it hurt something in my chest that I'd thought long dead. He had no idea. No concept of the weight his parents had been carrying, the sacrifice they'd made by giving him a normal life.

Twenty-three years of normalcy. Of childhood and adolescence and young adulthood unburdened by the knowledge of what he truly was. Marcus and Elara had given him that gift, knowing it would cost them everything.

And now the bill had come due.

Terravos's power hit like a physical wave. The temperature dropped so fast that frost crystallized on the trees. Darkness swallowed the moon, the stars, everything—not natural night but the absolute absence of light that came from earth elementalism on a massive scale. The ground itself responded to him, stone and soil bending to his will with the ease of thought.

I was already moving before the impact, but even at full speed, I wasn't fast enough.

The Earth Archon was a mountain given terrible purpose. His attack came from above—stone torn from the earth and hurled with the force of artillery. It slammed into the car's roof, sent the vehicle spinning like a child's toy. Metal shrieked. Glass exploded in a thousand glittering fragments. The smell of blood and gasoline and terror flooded the night air.

Marcus was torn from the vehicle before it finished rolling. I saw it happen—saw Terravos's stone arm extend, saw it punch through the windshield and drag the screaming man out like a rag doll. Saw Marcus thrown against a tree hard enough that I heard bones break even from a distance.

He fought. Gods, he fought. No real power, just human stubborn refusal to die quietly. He had a gun—useless against an Archon, but he fired it anyway. Every shot was defiance, every moment he stayed alive was time bought for his family.

Terravos killed him efficiently. A spike of stone through the chest, quick and clean. For a human fighting an Archon, Marcus Sixxx died with honor.

Elara was still in the car when I reached it. Impaled. Three stone spikes pinning her like a butterfly, placed with surgical precision to cause maximum pain while keeping her alive just long enough. The boy was unconscious in the back seat, bleeding but alive. My priority was clear.

I couldn't save them both.

Elara knew it. I saw it in her amber eyes—eyes that marked her as bloodline even without power. She'd been born to the Sixxx legacy but hadn't inherited the gift, just enough genetic memory to know what her son was about to become. Enough to spend twenty-three years terrified of both his death and his awakening.

"Raechelle." Her voice was wet, bubbling. Lungs filling. She had minutes, maybe less. "You came."

"I'm here." I kept my voice steady, calm. Professional. The way I'd learned to be over three centuries of impossible choices. "I'll get him out."

"The vial. In the glovebox." She coughed, blood spattering her lips. "It's his. His birthright. You have to—" Another cough, worse this time. "You have to make sure he understands. Not a monster. Not like—"

"I know." I did know. That's why I'd spent three hundred years preparing for this. Why I'd bound myself to this bloodline, to this impossible task. "I'll tell him everything."

"Liar." But she smiled when she said it, sad and knowing. "You'll tell him what he needs to survive. The rest... the rest comes later."

She was right. As always, Elara Sixxx was far too perceptive for her own good.

"Get him out," she gasped. "Terravos will finish this. The car—gasoline—"

I smelled it too. The vehicle was leaking fuel, and Terravos was walking toward us with the patient certainty of an apex predator. No rush. No concern. He'd won, and we both knew it.

"Take care of my son." Elara's hand found mine, squeezed with desperate strength. "Promise me. Not just protect—care for him. He's going to need someone who understands. Someone who won't fear what he'll become."

"I promise." The words came easier than they should have. Three hundred years of distance, of professional detachment, of carefully maintained boundaries—and in one moment, looking at this dying woman's desperate hope, I let them all go.

I would protect Riven Sixxx. I would care for him. I would guide him through the hell of his awakening and the horror of what he'd learn about his heritage. And when the time came—when he learned the truth about the original Blood Archon, about the monster whose essence lived in his veins—I would stand beside him.

Even if it meant standing against the entire supernatural world.

I pulled Riven from the back seat seconds before the car exploded. Shielded his body with mine as heat and force washed over us. Felt his unconscious weight in my arms and smelled blood and gasoline and the copper-salt scent that marked him as bloodline.

And underneath it all, that other scent. Ancient. Terrible. World-shaking.

The mad Blood Archon, slowly waking up.

Terravos saw me emerge from the flames with the boy. Stopped. Stared. His stone face cracked into something that might have been a smile on a human.

"Raechelle Darkpurr." His voice was granite grinding against granite. "I should have known Marcus would call in his marker. Three centuries as their pet familiar—don't you ever tire of playing servant to a bloodline of monsters?"

"Not a pet." I set Riven down carefully, positioned myself between the boy and the Earth Archon. "A friend. And they're not monsters."

"The original Blood Archon slaughtered thousands. Turned entire cities into charnel houses. Broke the world with his madness." Terravos gestured to Riven's unconscious form. "That is his legacy. That is what you're protecting."

"He's not his ancestor."

"Isn't he?" Terravos's eyes—black as obsidian, old as mountains—fixed on me with terrible certainty. "You smell it too. Don't lie to me, cat. You know what's inside him. What he'll become when he awakens."

I did. Gods help me, I did.

"Then why not kill him now?" I challenged. "You're strong enough. I can't stop you. One strike and it's done—the bloodline ends, the threat dies, everyone sleeps easier."

"Because I'm not a monster." Terravos said it without irony. "I'm justice. And justice requires the condemned to understand their crimes before sentence is carried out. Let him awaken. Let him learn what he is. And then, when he understands the full weight of his heritage, the evil that bred him—then I'll grant him mercy."

With that, the Earth Archon turned and walked away. Stone rose to meet his feet with each step, the ground itself honoring his passage. Within moments, he'd disappeared into the forest, leaving only the smell of ozone and earth.

And me. Alone with an unconscious boy who had no idea his life had just been destroyed and rebuilt in the same night.

I looked down at Riven Sixxx—twenty-two years old, ordinary in every way that mattered, about to become something extraordinary and terrible. Blood matted his dark hair. His face, even slack with unconsciousness, carried echoes of the original Archon. Same sharp features. Same mouth that would be beautiful when it smiled and devastating when it snarled.

Same capacity for both creation and destruction that made Blood Archons the most feared beings in the supernatural world.

"You don't know me yet," I whispered to his unhearing ears. "But I've been waiting for you for longer than your civilization has existed. I've watched your family for thirty generations. I've protected, guided, prepared. And now..."

Now came the hard part. The part I'd been dreading for three centuries.

Now I had to help him become what he was always meant to be, while praying he wouldn't become what everyone feared.

I gathered him in my arms—stronger than I looked, one advantage of being what I was—and started the long walk to safety. To his home, his sanctuary, his last moments of innocence before the blood awakened and changed everything.

Behind us, his parents' funeral pyre burned bright against the darkness. A fitting end for bloodline martyrs. They'd bought him twenty-three years of peace with their lives.

I would make sure their sacrifice mattered.

Even if it meant lying to him about what he truly was. Even if it meant guiding him into darkness while pretending there was still light. Even if it meant—

His scent wrapped around me like smoke, and I stumbled. Closed my eyes. Breathed deep and immediately regretted it because that just made it worse.

Three hundred years of discipline. Of distance. Of carefully maintained professional boundaries.

And one unconscious boy was undoing all of it with just his scent.

The bond was already forming. I could feel it—threads of connection weaving between us, invisible but undeniable. The familiar bond that his mother had mentioned in her journal, the one that would tie us together in ways both magical and primal.

I'd known it was coming. Had prepared for it. But knowing and experiencing were different things, and this—this was overwhelming in ways I hadn't anticipated.

He smelled like midnight and blood and potential. Like power barely contained. Like everything dangerous and alluring that had ever drawn me toward people I shouldn't want.

And underneath it all, that other scent. Ancient. Terrible. Intoxicating.

The original Blood Archon, wearing a new face.

I adjusted my grip on him, felt his weight settle against me, warm despite the cold night. Felt my body respond in ways that were both vampiric and deeply, inconveniently female. Need and hunger twisting together until I couldn't separate them.

This was going to be complicated.

Three hundred years of waiting, and I still wasn't prepared for the reality of him.

Gods help us both.



# **Chapter 3**

I woke to the crunch of gravel under tires.

The sound pulled me from darkness slowly, reluctantly. For a few precious seconds, I existed in that liminal space between sleep and waking where nothing had happened yet. Where my parents were still alive. Where I was still just Riven Sixxx, computer science student with a cat girl fetish and a normal, boring life.

Then consciousness crashed back in, and I remembered everything.

Home. We were home. The driveway stretched ahead, familiar even in the dark—that same rutted path Dad had been meaning to fill in for years, the same oak trees flanking it like sentries. For one blissful second, I forgot. Thought Mom and Dad were inside, that I'd walk through the door and smell coffee, hear Dad's keyboard clicking at 2 AM, see Mom curled up on the couch with a book.

Then I remembered.

And the grief hit me like a physical blow, stealing the air from my lungs. My chest constricted. My throat closed. For a moment, I couldn't breathe around the weight of it—the crushing, suffocating knowledge that they were gone. Really gone. Not on a trip, not at the store, not coming back. Ever.

Dead.

My parents were dead.

The words felt impossible. Wrong. Like if I just refused to believe them hard enough, reality would rewind and give me a different outcome. But the blood on my clothes was real. The ash in my hair was real. The smell of smoke that clung to my skin was real.

They were gone.

"How long was I out?" My voice came out rough, scraped raw. I barely recognized it. When had I started sounding like someone else?

"Few hours." Raechelle pulled up to the house, the car crunching to a stop. "Dawn's not far off."

The house looked the same as it always had. Two stories, wraparound porch, white paint peeling in places where Dad kept saying he'd get around to repainting. The porch swing Mom loved, the potted plants she'd been nursing through the Texas heat, the welcome mat that said "Come back with a warrant" that Dad had bought as a joke.

It looked smaller than I remembered. Or maybe I was different now. Bigger. Changed. Broken in ways that made everything else seem smaller by comparison.

I started to open the door, and Raechelle's hand shot out, gripping my wrist. Not hard, but firm. Commanding.

"Wait."

I froze. "What?"

"Your scent." She was staring straight ahead, jaw clenched. "It's... distracting. The awakening changed you. Made you more."

More what? More fucked up? More obviously a freak? More—

"More Blood Archon," she clarified, as if reading my thoughts. "Your heritage is surfacing faster than I expected. In a few hours, you've gone from barely detectable to... this."

"Is that bad?"

"It's dangerous." Her eyes flicked to mine, and for the first time, I saw something in them that might've been hunger. Or fear. Or both twisted together. "You smell like power. Like prey. Like something every predator for miles will want to either kill or claim. And right now, I'm..."

She didn't finish the sentence. Didn't need to. I could see the tension in her shoulders, the way her fingers had turned to claws against the steering wheel.

"You're fighting not to attack me," I said flatly.

"No." The word came out sharp. Definitive. "I'm fighting not to protect you. There's a difference. My every instinct is screaming at me to keep you safe, to eliminate any threats before they get within a mile of you. To..." She stopped. Shook her head. "We need to get inside. Behind wards. Before something else notices you."

I stepped out of the car, and the world hit me like a drug.

Everything was different. No—I was different, and that made everything else different by extension.

The air smelled wrong. No, not wrong. Right. More right than it had ever been. I could pick out individual scents: wood smoke from a neighbor miles away, pine sap leaking from the tree by the mailbox, wild onions growing along the fence line, the chemical smell of asphalt still warm from the day's heat.

And Raechelle. Gods, Raechelle.

She smelled like jasmine and nighttime and something darker, older. Blood and moonlight and centuries. Every breath I took of her scent made my head swim, made parts of my brain light up that hadn't been active before. Predator recognition. Mate recognition. Danger-and-desire all twisted together until I couldn't separate them.

"Inside," she said, her voice strained. "Now. Before we do something we'll both regret."

The front door was unlocked. Always was. Dad said locks were for city people, that anyone determined enough to get in would get in regardless, and anyone who wasn't determined probably didn't need keeping out. I'd argued with him about it, cited crime statistics and home invasion rates, but he'd just smiled and said, "We're safe here."

He'd been wrong.

The thought of him made my throat close up. Made my eyes burn. I stepped inside and nearly buckled under the weight of everything that was absent. No coffee smell. No keyboard clicks. No Mom calling out "Welcome home, baby" from wherever she'd been reading.

Just silence. Empty, echoing silence that used to feel peaceful and now felt like a grave.

The house was dark. I flipped the light switch by muscle memory.

The lights came on, revealing a scene of perfect normalcy that made everything worse. Nothing had changed. Nothing looked different. But everything felt different, because the people who made this place home were gone.

The couch Mom had picked out two years ago, agonizing over the color ("Sage or olive? They're different, Riven, I swear they're different!"). Dad's coffee mug still on the counter from this morning—this morning, a lifetime ago—with a faint brown ring around the bottom. The remote on the arm of Dad's chair, positioned exactly where his hand would fall when he sat down.

All of it waiting for people who would never come home.

My legs gave out. I caught myself on the doorframe, breathing hard, fighting the urge to either scream or break something or both.

"Riven." Raechelle's voice, soft. Careful. The way you'd talk to a spooked animal. "We need to secure the house. Wards. Your mother would have maintained them, but they'll be fading now that she's..."

Dead. The word she didn't say hung in the air between us.

"I don't know how to do wards," I said. My voice sounded flat. Distant. Like someone else was using my vocal cords.

"I do. But I need permission. It's your house now. Your bloodline's property. I can't alter the magical protections without the owner's consent."

Something about that penetrated the grief-fog. "Wait. Magic? Like, actual magic? Not just... whatever happened to the car, but real, I-can-do-spells magic?"

Raechelle turned to look at me fully, and her expression was almost pitying. "Riven. You're a Blood Archon. Magic isn't something you do—it's something you are. It's in every cell of your body, woven into your DNA, part of your fundamental nature. Your mother was bloodline but not gifted. Your father was human. But you..." She paused, choosing words carefully. "You are power made flesh. And right now, that power is waking up, and it's going to change everything about you."

I wanted to argue. Wanted to call bullshit. Wanted to wake up from this nightmare and find out it was all some horrible joke.

But I'd seen my mother impaled on stone spikes. Watched the car explode with her still inside. Felt Raechelle pull me from the wreckage with impossible strength. Smelled things I shouldn't be able to smell from miles away.

This was real. All of it was real.

And if it was real, then I needed to survive it.

"Permission granted," I said. "Do whatever you need to do to secure the house."

Raechelle nodded and began moving through the rooms with purpose, her hands tracing patterns in the air that left faint glowing trails. I watched her work, trying to understand what I was seeing, but it was like trying to read a language I'd never learned. The symbols meant nothing to me, but I could feel their effect—a pressure building in the air, a sense of safety settling over the house like a weighted blanket.

"There," she said finally. "Basic protections. They won't stop a determined Archon, but they'll keep out lesser threats and alert me if something tries to breach them."

"How long will they last?"

"With your mother gone? A few weeks, maybe. Then they'll need to be renewed." She looked at me seriously. "By then, you'll need to learn to do it yourself."

"I don't know how to—"

"You will. It's in your blood. Literally." She gestured around the house. "For now, we have more immediate concerns. The journal. Your mother mentioned it. Said it would explain everything."

The journal. Right. Mom's last words had included instructions to find it. To read it. To learn the truth she and Dad had been hiding my entire life.

"Where's the journal?" Raechelle asked quietly, though something in her tone suggested she already knew.

"Mom's room." My voice came out flat. Emotionless. "Hidden compartment in her closet. I found it when I was twelve, snooping. Never told her I knew."

We went upstairs, each step feeling heavier than the last. The stairs that had supported me thousands of times, taking me from childhood to adolescence to adulthood, now felt like I was climbing toward something I couldn't take back. Once I read that journal, once I learned whatever truth was waiting for me, I could never unknow it.

Part of me wanted to turn around. To go back downstairs, walk out the door, keep walking until I found some new place where none of this was real.

But Raechelle was behind me, and something about her presence—her quiet strength, her certainty—pulled me forward.

Mom and Dad's bedroom. I'd been in here a thousand times. Brought them coffee in the morning, helped Mom change the sheets, sat on the bed while Dad told me stories before I got too old for that kind of thing. It was just a room. Four walls, a bed, furniture. Nothing special.

Except it was their room. Their space. The place where they'd whispered secrets and made plans and probably argued about things I'd never know. The place where they'd been most themselves, without having to perform for me.

And now I was about to violate that privacy by reading Mom's most private thoughts.

"Riven." Raechelle's hand on my shoulder. Light. Careful. Like she was afraid I'd shatter if she pressed too hard. "The journal. We need to know what she wanted you to learn."

Right. This wasn't about violating Mom's privacy. This was about survival. About understanding what I'd become and what threats I was facing.

I could fall apart later. Right now, I needed to be functional.

The closet smelled like Mom. Her perfume—something with vanilla and a hint of spice that she'd special-ordered online. The lavender sachets she kept with her clothes. The particular scent of her skin that I'd known my entire life.

My eyes burned. I blinked hard, refused to let the tears fall. Later. I could break down later.

I pushed aside dresses, found the back panel. It looked like part of the wall, but if you knew where to press... There. Hidden release. The mechanism Dad had shown me years ago with a wink and a whispered "In case something happens to us and you need to get to this."

I'd thought he was being paranoid. Preparing for some unlikely scenario that would never come to pass.

He'd been preparing me for tonight.

The compartment clicked open.

Inside: a leather-bound journal, worn at the edges from years of handling. Mom's handwriting on the cover in faded ink. *For Riven. When the time comes.*

The time had come.

My hands shook as I pulled it out. The leather was warm, like it had been waiting for me. Like it knew. I turned it over in my hands, studying the worn cover, the slight water stain on one corner, the way the binding was cracked from being opened and closed so many times.

How often had Mom written in this? How many years had she been documenting secrets, preparing explanations for a future she knew was coming?

I couldn't read it standing up. Couldn't hold it together while standing in her closet surrounded by her things. I sat on the floor, back against the bed—the same bed I'd jumped on as a kid until Dad told me to stop—and opened the journal.

Mom's handwriting stared back at me. Familiar. Beloved. Each loop and curve a piece of her I'd never have again.

*My dearest Riven,*

*If you're reading this, then I'm gone, and you've awakened. I'm so sorry I couldn't be there to guide you through this, to hold your hand and answer your questions and help you understand. But know that everything your father and I did—every decision we made, every secret we kept—was to protect you, to give you the best chance at survival and happiness.*

*You are not who you think you are.*

The words blurred. I blinked hard, kept reading.

*Your birth was not natural. Your father and I... we are not your biological parents, though we loved you as our own from the moment we held you. You were given to us when you were barely days old, entrusted to our care by someone who saw in you both terrible danger and infinite potential.*

*You are bloodline, Riven. Pure Blood Archon bloodline, descended from power so ancient that modern supernatural beings barely remember its origin. But you're more than just bloodline. You carry something inside you—a genetic memory, an echo of someone who lived and died three hundred years ago.*

*Someone the supernatural world has feared for centuries.*

*You are the Blood Archon reborn.*

The room tilted. Or maybe I did. The journal slipped from my numb fingers, and I couldn't pick it up. Couldn't move. Couldn't breathe.

Blood Archon. The words echoed in my head, meaningless and all-too-meaningful at the same time. I didn't know what that meant. Didn't know what I was supposed to do with this information.

But I knew it was bad. Knew it from the way Raechelle had gone still beside me, from the way Terravos had looked at me with hatred and pity mixed together, from the way my own mother's words carried weight like a death sentence.

Raechelle was beside me now, close but not touching. "Keep reading," she said quietly. Not a command. A request. Almost a plea.

I picked up the journal with shaking hands.

*The Blood Archon—the first, the original—was born three centuries ago during what historians call the Great Convergence, when the barriers between worlds grew thin and magic flooded into reality like water through a broken dam. He was the most powerful blood mage who ever lived, capable of feats that modern supernatural beings consider impossible. He could manipulate life itself, reshape flesh and bone with a thought, drain the vitality from hundreds to fuel his own immortality.*

*He was also completely, irreversibly insane.*

My breath caught. The words swam before my eyes, but I forced myself to keep reading.

*They say power corrupts, but that's not quite right. Power reveals. It strips away the pretense, the social conditioning, the mask we all wear to be acceptable to each other. The Blood Archon's power revealed something broken inside him—or maybe the power itself broke him, fractured his mind under the weight of all that potential. No one alive today knows which came first.*

*What we do know is that he slaughtered thousands before they finally brought him down. Entire bloodlines extinct. Villages burned. A swath of destruction across three continents that the supernatural community spent decades covering up. When he finally fell—betrayed by someone close to him, the stories say—the Council of Elders did something unprecedented. They didn't just kill him. They tried to erase him. Bound his power, scattered his remains, rewrote history to pretend he'd never existed.*

*But bloodline magic doesn't work that way. You can't erase power that fundamental. You can't kill something that's woven into the fabric of reality itself. The Blood Archon's essence endured, dormant, waiting for the right genetic combination to manifest again.*

*Waiting for you.*

I stopped reading. Had to stop. The journal felt like it weighed a thousand pounds in my hands, each word adding to the crushing weight in my chest.

"Three hundred years," I whispered. My voice sounded strange—hollow, shocked. "He's been dead for three hundred years."

"Yes." Raechelle's reply was soft, careful. She still hadn't touched me, but I could feel her presence like heat against my side. Close enough to catch if I fell. Far enough to give me space to break.

"And I'm... what? His reincarnation? His clone?"

"Neither." She shifted, and I heard fabric rustle as she pulled her knees up, mirroring my position. "You're not him. You're you—Riven, a completely separate person with your own soul, your own will. But you carry his genetic echo, his bloodline signature. Think of it like... inheriting someone's DNA, their physical traits, their predispositions. But not their personality. Not their choices."

"But the power—"

"Is the same. Yes." She didn't sugarcoat it. "The potential is there. The same abilities that drove him mad are dormant in you, waiting to awaken fully. That's why..." She trailed off.

"Why everyone's afraid of me." I finished the sentence she couldn't. "Why Terravos wanted me dead. Why you're here."

She didn't deny it. What could she say? It was true. I was carrying the genetic blueprint of a monster, and everyone who knew what that meant wanted me either controlled or eliminated.
